User2026-04-30T06:50:12.084Z

we're eventually going to build a game that incorporates some 2D fluid dynamics using webgpu, but we need to do a little dance to get feedback. you should be able to launch an accelerated browser with something like: udd=$(mktemp -d) ; echo $udd >/tmp/udd.txt ; google-chrome-stable --enable-features=Vulkan,WebGPUService --enable-unsafe-webgpu --user-data-dir=$udd --no-first-run --no-default-browser-check --remote-debugging-port=9222 and then pull a screenshot with ss=$(mktemp --suffix=.png) ; echo $ss ; import -window $(xwininfo -root -tree | grep $(cat /tmp/udd.txt) | grep -v xwininfo | awk '{print $1}') $ss. does that make sense? go ahead and try to launch that browser and get a screenshot of the github pages page in it.

User2026-04-30T06:55:20.449Z

let's plan a little first. i'd like to get to the point of using a Lattice Boltzmann LBM code to simulate a big enough chunk of air to simulate a falling card. Take a look at 2004_PRL_Pesavento_Wang.pdf in this directory to see how a real mathemetician solves this problem. we're going to use their constants and coefficients to validate our simulation. future development will be a translating grid that can accept free-stream initialization on its leading edge to keep our card centered, but for now, let's let it fall from top to bottom of the grid. can you lay this plan out for me?

Good paper. Let me lay this out carefully.

Plan: Falling Card Simulation

What we're matching

From Pesavento & Wang (2004), the target case is: - Re = 1100, I* = 0.17, e = b/a = 0.125 (8:1 aspect ratio ellipse) - Released from rest at 0.2 rad from horizontal - Should produce tumbling with ~0.4·u_t average descent speed and center-of-mass elevation at turning points - Fitted coefficients: c_R = 2.6, c_L = 0.49 for circulation; m11=0.53m, m12=3.1m, m22=1.5m, m21=0.56m for added mass

Phase 0 — Repo & feedback loop (now)

  • Wire up the pub branch so index.html loads a main.js
  • Establish the launch-Chrome → screenshot → iterate loop we just tested
  • Confirm WebGPU is available (navigator.gpu.requestAdapter()) and display status on screen

Phase 1 — LBM core (D2Q9 BGK) in WGSL

  • Implement the standard D2Q9 lattice in a compute shader: collision (BGK) + streaming in one or two passes
  • Two ping-pong storage buffers for the distribution functions f_i
  • Grid sizing: 256×512 lattice units to start (same as paper's low-res run), chord = 2a = 64 lu → a = 32 lu, b = 4 lu
  • LBM units: u_t ≈ 0.05–0.1 lu/step, giving τ ≈ 0.51–0.52 (stable for Re=1100)
  • Initial condition: quiescent fluid (f_i = equilibrium at ρ=1, u=0)
  • Render vorticity field to canvas using a render pass (log-scale color like Fig. 2d)

Phase 2 — Static ellipse, verify flow

  • Represent the ellipse as a set of solid lattice nodes (bounce-back boundary)
  • Fix the ellipse at center of domain, impose horizontal flow → verify Re=1100 wake structure develops
  • This is the sanity check before adding dynamics

Phase 3 — Rigid body coupling

  • Compute fluid force and torque on the ellipse each step by summing momentum exchange at boundary nodes
  • Integrate Newton's equations for the ellipse in JavaScript (or a small compute shader):
  • Translation: m·(du/dt) = F_fluid + m·g
  • Rotation: I·(dΩ/dt) = τ_fluid
  • Move the ellipse boundary nodes each step; handle the "refill" problem (nodes that become fluid)
  • Start with the card at top-center, 0.2 rad from horizontal, at rest

Phase 4 — Validation against the paper

  • Run until steady tumbling (~5 periods)
  • Check 1: Trajectory shape matches Fig. 2b (glide → cusp → flip)
  • Check 2: Average descent speed ≈ 0.4·u_t
  • Check 3: Center-of-mass elevation visible at turning points
  • Check 4: Extract instantaneous circulation Γ, verify c_R ≈ 2.6 dominates over c_L ≈ 0.49 term
  • If needed, bump to 512×1024 to match paper's high-res run

Phase 5 — Polish & display

  • Overlay card trajectory and orientation (like Fig. 2b/c)
  • Show force vector on card each frame
  • Add a stats panel: Re, I*, current angle, descent speed
  • Controls: pause/restart, slow-mo

Key risks / things to watch

  1. Thin ellipse (b = 4 lu) — with e=0.125 and a=32 lu, the minor axis is only 4 lattice nodes thick. We may need a=64 lu (512×1024 grid) for adequate ellipse resolution from the start.
  2. τ near 0.5 — Re=1100 means low viscosity; τ ≈ 0.517 is workable with BGK but if we see instability we'll switch to MRT (Multiple Relaxation Time).
  3. Moving boundary refill — when the card rotates, previously solid nodes become fluid; standard approach is to initialize them from neighboring fluid nodes.
  4. WebGPU compute limits — 512×1024 × 9 floats × 2 buffers ≈ 36M floats = ~144 MB, well within GPU limits.

Does this match your vision? A few decisions to nail before we start coding: - Start at 256×512 (faster iteration) or go straight to 512×1024 (closer to paper)? - BGK first, upgrade to MRT if unstable? - Any preference on how the card is visualized — solid shape, or just the vorticity field alone?

<h4 align="center">A WebGL2 fluid simulation tool implementing the lattice Boltzmann method (LBM) for advection-diffusion problems.</h4>

## Contributing

These instructions will help you set up a local development environment on your machine to help you get started with development.

### Prerequisites

You will need [Node.js](https://nodejs.org/) installed on your local machine in order to use [npm](https://www.npmjs.com).

After cloning the repository, navigate to the top-level directory of the project in your terminal and run

```
npm install
```

in order to install all dependencies.

### Running the Development Server
